                                          Abstract

Machine Learning Models that Connect Mind and World
                                        Weikang Qiu
                                           2026

    In contemporary machine learning systems, language often mediates interaction be-
tween people and modalities. Text-to-image generators, vision-language models, and voice
assistants, for example, assume that human intent can be expressed in words before the
system interprets or acts on it. Yet verbal expression is serial and limited in bandwidth,
and some mental content is difficult to put into words. RepQA, our benchmark for pa-
tient education question answering, provides a narrower motivation from the system side:
methods can improve readability, but simplified answers may still omit important content.
These limitations motivate this dissertation to explore a complementary interface between
mind and world that does not require people to articulate their intent first. This problem
divides into three questions: (1) how can machine learning uncover neural organization
that is not specified in advance, and how can established neural priors in turn be built into
machine-learning models; (2) what representation space and structure can best connect
brain activity to the world, and how can the necessary supervision be assembled when
neural data are scarce; and (3) how can neural signals be coupled to embodied systems?
    For the first question, we study a two-way exchange between machine learning and neu-
ral priors: machine learning can discover neural organization that is not specified in advance,
while established neural organization can guide the design of machine-learning models.
HyBRiD realizes the first direction by learning outcome-relevant, high-order relationships
among brain regions as weighted hyperedges rather than restricting connectivity to pairwise
edges; a multi-head drop-bottleneck makes a maximally-informative-minimally-redundant
objective tractable, allowing the hyperedges to be learned end-to-end instead of enumerated
and pruned in advance. In the other direction, our neuro-informed attention encoder aggre-
gates subject-specific sets of responsive voxels with learnable queries, while its keys contain
only voxel position and multi-atlas parcellation embeddings, separating measured fMRI
activity from spatial and parcellation priors that can be shared across subjects. FA-EEG
likewise uses frame averaging to make the known mirror symmetry between left and right
motor imagery exact for any backbone rather than relearning it from scarce trials. This
transfer from neuroscience to machine learning also extends beyond models of neural
signals: SWA identifies small-world organization in functional networks of language-model
attention heads, relates that organization to fluid reasoning, and preserves it when allocating
sparsity during pruning.
    For the second question, we compare candidate alignment spaces and find that, among
those tested, fMRI aligns more closely with language-model semantic representations than
with the visual latents conventionally used for reconstruction. Brain instruction tuning
                                                                                          2


then creates supervision without new scanning by joining fMRI recordings to a decade of
existing annotations through their shared stimulus images. The resulting corpus of 980,610
conversations spans four families of brain function: perception, memory and knowledge,
language and symbols, and complex reasoning. A decoder trained on this corpus answers
open-ended questions in a unified format, improves general decoding, and generalizes
to a held-out subject without finetuning. Reconstruction reveals a further issue: unified
representations can entangle objects with their attributes. PRISM structures the alignment
target into objects, attributes, and relations and generates scenes object by object. D-Edit
completes the generative side by assigning one soft prompt to each image item and using
grouped cross-attention to confine each region to its own prompt, supporting item-level
text-, mask-, and image-based editing and object removal.
    For the third question, supervision is again the bottleneck: conventional motor-imagery
datasets are built from cue-locked, hand-labeled trials. Our Mind World Model makes both
sides of the learning problem scalable. Synchronized EEG and egocentric video can be
recorded continuously without trial or action labels. A mind model maps current EEG
to an action code and learns only from an action-conditioned world model’s next-frame
prediction error. In the other direction, that EEG-derived code turns adjacent video frames
into action-conditioned training examples without recorded actions; because it is computed
without access to the target frame, it also prevents the action channel from simply carrying
the future observation. DySta tackles inference-time scaling in vision-language-action
policies. It separates visual tokens by temporal persistence, retains a single copy and
KV cache for static tokens, and refreshes them through a learned recache gate, reducing
both context length and per-step computation while improving control success. PHIL
complements this fast loop with a slower one, learning a cost-to-go that truncates lookahead
when neural signals specify goals rather than trajectories.

2.2   Overview of the R EP QA benchmark. Top: dataset construction — patient-
      education QA pairs are collected from 27 online public health resources and
      filtered by domain experts to suit lay users, yielding 533 expert-grounded
      QA pairs (each with an auto-generated multiple-choice counterpart) that
      span four categories (Suggestion, Fact, Definition, and Rationale). Bottom:
      evaluation — given a question, an LLM agent generates a free-form re-
      sponse, which is scored by three readability metrics (Flesch–Kincaid grade,
      professional language, and Yngve depth) and an informativeness metric
      (the informative cosine between the response and the expert reference
      answer). . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .    10

3.1   We identify high-order relationships of brain regions, where hyperedge
      structures and weights possess strong relevance to cognition (maximal
      informativeness). Meanwhile, they contain the least irrelevant information.     26




                                        viii
3.2   Overview of the H Y BR I D pipeline when the total number of hyperedges
      K = 3. Hyperedge are in distinct colors for clarity. The C ONSTRUCTOR
      identifies hyperedges in the hypergraph, where regions are nodes. The
      W EIGHTER computes a weight for each hyperedge. These weights, repre-
      senting strengths of hyperedges, are expected to be informative in terms
      of our target Y . There are two separate phases after obtaining weights of
      hyperedges: 1) Training. The model’s parameters are trained under the
      supervision of Y ; 2) Evaluation. The output weights, as well as pairwise
      weights, are fed into the CPM (see Appendix ??). . . . . . . . . . . . . .        30
3.3   Architecture details of the C ONSTRUCTOR and the W EIGHTER when the
      number of nodes N = 6 and the number of hyperedges K = 3. At a
      high level, the C ONSTRUCTOR learns the hyperedge structure by masking
      nodes. The W EIGHTER computes the weight of each hyperedge based on
      the hyperedge’s member nodes and their features. . . . . . . . . . . . . .        32
3.4   Hyperedge profiles. (a) Hyperedge degree distribution of learned hyper-

      Comparison between the number of hyperedges and pairwise edges under
      different significance thresholds. The total number of hyperedges is 32.

4.1   The overview of our method. MindLLM is equipped with a subject-agnostic
      fMRI encoder and an off-the-shelf LLM. MindLLM is trained on multiple
      subjects with varying input shapes and an instruction-tuning dataset, aiming
      to encode different facets of semantic information in fMRI. After training,
      MindLLM is capable of various text decoding tasks. One application is
      that the decoded contents can be used to achieve neural control of existing
      systems that are not designed for it. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   42
4.2   Model Architecture. The fMRI encoder maps fMRI to a series of fMRI
      tokens through our proposed neuroscience-informed attention. The large
      language model, with both fMRI and text tokens, will be trained by brain
      instruction tuning. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   46

      means we employ an MLP to map the coordinates to the embeddings
      instead of positional encoding. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .     50

6.1  The editing pipeline of using D-Edit. The user first uploads an image which
     is segmented into several items. After finetuning DPMs, the user can do
     various types of control, including (a) replacing the model with another
     using a text prompt; (b) refining imperfect details caused by segmentation;
     (c) moving bags to the ground; (d) replacing the handbag with another one
     from a reference image; (e) reshaping handbag; (f) resizing the model and
     handbag; (g) removing background. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .         60

     only attends to the text prompt (two tokens) assigned to it. . . . . . . . .      61

                                          x
7.1   Framework Overview: PRISM generates structured text descriptions for each
      training image using a VLM to iteratively extract brain-aligned object attributes
      and relationships. These descriptions capture the image’s compositional and
      relational content and serve as supervision to train an encoder and fine-tune a
      language model to map fMRI signals into the text space. During inference, the
      model predicts descriptions from fMRI signals, which then guide a pre-trained
      diffusion model for object-centric image reconstruction. . . . . . . . . . . . .    77
7.2   Reconstructed images from different methods. The first column shows the original
      viewed images. The rest of the columns show the reconstructed images from
      different methods. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .      83

8.1   Our model solves two main challenges in existing VLAs with the proposed
      static-dynamic disentanglement. 1) By keeping one copy of static tokens
      across all timesteps, our model is able to squash observations of multiple
      steps to the model’s context; 2) By moving static tokens in front of all
      dynamic tokens, our model could reuse the KV-cache of previous timesteps
      during rollouts. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 92
8.2   Some of the existing methods Xu et al. [2025], Liu et al. [2025] that exploit
      to reuse information in previous frames ignore the problem that the static
      patches will be affected by attentions even if they are identical in the
      original pixel space. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 94
8.3   Model architecture overview. We illustrate the design using two levels of
      static cache. At each level, a recache gate determines whether the cached
      static tokens should be reused or refreshed. If the L1 cache is refreshed, the
      L2 cache is also forcibly refreshed. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 95

      from different trajectories form negative pairs. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 97

                                           xi
9.1   The goal is to navigate (find a path) from the start to the goal node. While
      BFS visits many nodes to find a start-to-goal path (left), one can use a
      heuristic based on the features of the nodes (e.g., Euclidean distance) on
      the graph to reduce the search effort (middle). We propose PHIL to learn a
      tailored search heuristic for a given graph, capable of reducing the number
      of visited nodes even further by exploiting the inductive biases of the graph
      (right). . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   107
9.2   Main components of PHIL: On the left, using a greedy mixture policy
      induced by the current version of our parameterized heuristic hθ and an
      oracle heuristic h∗ (i.e., a heuristic that correctly determines distances
      between nodes), we roll-out a search trajectory from the start node to the
      goal node. Each trajectory step contains a set of newly added fringe nodes
      with bounded random subsets of their 1-hop neighborhoods and their oracle
      (h∗ ) distances to the goal node. Trajectories are aggregated throughout the
      training procedure. On the right, we use truncated backpropagation through
      time on each collected trajectory to train hθ , where ĥ is the predicted
      distance between x2 and xg , and z2 is the updated state of the memory.
      Here, the memory captures the embedding of the graph visited so far. . . .       108
9.3   This figure demonstrates the core idea behind our IL algorithm. We present
      the roll-in phase on the left-hand side, where our policy is rolled in for t
      steps to obtain state st and embedding zt . On the right-hand side, we show
      the trajectory collection and training steps, where we aggregate the trajec-
      tory for downstream training (green) and use truncated backpropagation
      through time on the collected dataset (red). . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .     113

                                            xv
Chapter 1

                                 Introduction

1.1     Motivation
Language has become the default interface between people and multimodal machine
learning systems. Modern models can process and generate text, images, audio, and
video [Han et al., 2024, Radford et al., 2021, Liu et al., 2023b], yet communication across
these modalities is usually routed through words. In text-to-image generation, users describe
the desired result with a prompt [Rombach et al., 2022]; in vision-language models, an
image is interpreted through a linguistic question and answer [Liu et al., 2023b]; and in
voice assistants, spoken instructions are translated into actions. This design is natural and
useful, but it raises a basic question: should language be the only bridge between a person’s
internal state and a machine’s behavior?
    Language is powerful, but it can also be a narrow channel. Expression unfolds se-
quentially, so a person often has to compress a rich intention into a short prompt and then
refine it through several rounds of feedback. Some experiences are even harder to verbalize.
The abstract structure and affective intensity of Wassily Kandinsky’s Composition VII, for
example, can be perceived at once but resist a precise sentence-by-sentence description.
These examples do not make language obsolete. They motivate a complementary channel
that does not require every percept, intention, or internal state to be deliberately formulated
in words first.
    The same limitation can appear in the opposite direction, when a machine communicates
with a person. In patient education, an answer is useful only if a lay reader can understand
it without losing essential information. R EP QA, introduced in Chapter 2, shows that
current large language models often miss a requested reading level, while interventions
that improve readability can omit part of the original content. This is a narrow setting
rather than a universal test of language, but it illustrates the larger problem: having the right
information does not guarantee that it reaches the recipient intact.
    Motivated by these observations, this dissertation develops machine learning methods


                                               1
toward a more direct interface between mind and world. On the mind side, these methods
learn from neural signals and the organization of the brain. On the world side, semantic
and perceptual representations make neural information communicable, while embodied
models make decoded commands or goals actionable. Language can still serve as an
internal representation or an output format; the aim is to remove the requirement that a
person must first turn every intention into a verbal instruction.
    Such an interface could matter in three ways. First, models that identify meaningful
patterns in neural activity can help reveal how distributed brain systems support perception,
cognition, and behavior. They may also support the search for biomarkers, treatment-
response prediction, and rehabilitation monitoring, including research on Alzheimer’s
disease, depression, and multiple sclerosis [Li et al., 2015a, Siegle et al., 2006, Cerasa
et al., 2013]. Second, neural decoding can express parts of a person’s brain state as text or
reconstructed images. Existing studies have recovered the semantic content of perceived
and imagined speech, reconstructed viewed images, identified seen or imagined objects,
and predicted broad categories of visual imagery during sleep onset [Tang et al., 2023a,
Takagi and Nishimoto, 2023, Horikawa and Kamitani, 2017, Horikawa et al., 2013]. These
results remain partial, subject-specific, and task-dependent, but they point toward ways to
externalize aspects of internal thought, mental imagery, and dreams. Third, decoded neural
signals could provide commands or goals to external systems. Speech neuroprostheses
and neurally controlled robotic limbs already demonstrate the potential of this direction
for people with speech or motor impairments [Card et al., 2024, Hochberg et al., 2012]; in
the longer term, decoded intentions could also guide other assistive devices and embodied
agents.
    Realizing this interface raises three fundamental challenges. (1) Understanding the
brain requires learning from neural organization in both directions: machine learning
must discover meaningful structure that is not specified in advance, while established
neuroscience must provide useful inductive biases for models trained on scarce and variable
data. (2) Decoding brain states requires deciding which representation of the world is well
aligned with neural activity and learning that mapping from limited paired data, so that
recoverable content can be rendered as text or images. (3) Enabling interaction requires
connecting decoded commands or goals to systems that can act reliably: embodied agents
must retain relevant history, respond efficiently, and plan over longer horizons.


1.2     Contributions
This dissertation addresses these challenges through a sequence that moves from neural
signals, to semantic and perceptual representations, and finally to embodied interaction.
A common principle is to make useful structure explicit at each stage: higher-order and
anatomical structure when modeling the brain, semantic and compositional structure when


                                             2
decoding its state, and temporal and graph structure when acting in the world. The main
contributions are grouped as follows:
    • Understanding the brain with structure-aware models. I develop models that
      use the relationship between machine learning and neuroscience in both directions.
      H Y BR I D discovers outcome-relevant, high-order relationships among brain regions
      instead of restricting analysis to pairwise connections. A neuroscience-informed
      attention encoder then incorporates voxel location and brain parcellations into a
      subject-agnostic architecture. Together, these methods show how machine learning
      can uncover neural organization and how neural organization can guide model design
      (Chapters 3 and 4).

    • Decoding brain states through structured representations. I develop methods
      on both sides of the bridge between brain activity and the external world. Brain
      Instruction Tuning expands supervision by connecting fMRI recordings to existing
      annotations through shared stimulus images. PRISM studies which of several
      candidate representation spaces aligns most closely with fMRI and uses structured
      descriptions of objects, attributes, and relations for image reconstruction. D-Edit
      complements this decoding work on the generative side by providing item-level
      control over image content (Chapters 5–7).

    • Building control and planning components for interaction. I develop embodied
      models that can use information across time and translate goals into efficient behav-
      ior. DySta separates static and dynamic visual information to support multi-frame
      vision-language-action control with lower inference cost. PHIL learns graph-search
      heuristics with a memory of the explored environment, providing a complementary
      mechanism for longer-horizon planning. These components supply capabilities that
      an interactive neural interface would need after a command or goal has been decoded
      (Chapters 8 and 9).
    My long-term goal is to develop an additional communication channel between people
and machines, especially when speech is impossible, too slow, or unable to preserve the
relevant content. The work in this dissertation does not assume unrestricted access to a
person’s thoughts, nor does it present a fully integrated brain-to-robot system. Instead, it
establishes and evaluates several of the modeling, representation, control, and planning
components needed to move from understanding neural signals toward useful interaction
with the world.


1.3     Thesis Outline
The dissertation begins with a prologue and then proceeds in three parts: the foundation
for modeling brain signals, the bridge from signals to representations, and the embodied


                                             3
systems that turn representations into interaction. This order follows the path from under-
standing the brain, to decoding aspects of its state, to building systems capable of acting on
decoded information.
     Prologue. Chapter 2 introduces R EP QA, a benchmark for evaluating readability in
patient education question answering. The chapter asks whether large language models
can write at a requested reading level, infer the level required by a question, and improve
readability without sacrificing informativeness. The results show that the models often
fail to meet requested reading levels and that readability-oriented post-training can discard
useful content. This chapter provides a concrete example of information being lost in the
machine-to-human direction and motivates the search for complementary interfaces.
     PART I establishes the foundation for learning from brain signals. Its two chapters
examine the relationship between machine learning and neural structure in opposite direc-
tions: the first uses machine learning to discover organization in the brain, and the second
builds established organization into a neural encoder.
     Chapter 3 introduces H Y BR I D, a method for discovering high-order relationships
among brain regions. Existing approaches usually represent functional connectivity with
pairwise edges or select higher-order candidates before learning, which can miss dis-
tributed patterns that matter for cognition. H Y BR I D instead represents these relationships
as weighted hyperedges and learns them end to end through a maximally-informative-
minimally-redundant objective and a multi-head drop-bottleneck. The resulting structures
improve phenotypic prediction and provide interpretable evidence about the role of higher-
order brain organization.
     Chapter 4 develops a neuroscience-informed attention encoder for subject-agnostic
fMRI modeling. Responsive voxels differ in number and spatial distribution across people,
making a fixed input architecture difficult to share across subjects. The encoder uses
learnable queries to aggregate these irregular voxel sets, while its keys encode voxel
position and multiple brain parcellations and its values carry the measured fMRI activity.
Separating transferable spatial and anatomical priors from subject-specific measurements
improves decoding and supports generalization to an unseen subject.
     PART II moves from modeling neural signals to aligning them with representations of
the external world. The three chapters study how to assemble broad supervision, how to
structure generative control, and how to choose a useful representation space for reconstruc-
tion.
     Chapter 5 introduces Brain Instruction Tuning. Because paired fMRI–text data are
scarce and expensive to collect, the method uses shared stimulus images to connect existing
fMRI recordings with annotations from fifteen external datasets. The resulting corpus
contains 980,610 conversations spanning perception and scene understanding, memory and
knowledge, language and symbols, and complex reasoning. This construction allows a
single decoder to be trained for a broad family of questions without requiring new scanning
for every task.


                                              4
    Chapter 6 studies the generative side of the bridge independently of brain signals.
Existing diffusion-based editors often entangle the control of one object with the rest of
an image. D-Edit assigns a separate learned prompt to each image item and introduces
grouped cross-attention so that each region is controlled by its associated prompt. This
design supports text-, image-, and mask-guided editing as well as item removal within one
framework.
    Chapter 7 returns to brain decoding and asks which representation should connect fMRI
to image generation. Among the vision, language, and vision-language spaces evaluated,
the experiments find stronger alignment with language-model semantic representations.
PRISM therefore represents a scene through its objects, attributes, and relations and
reconstructs the image object by object. This structured bridge improves semantic fidelity
and also supports retrieval through decoded keywords.
    PART III turns to the systems that would act on decoded commands or goals. Its
two chapters study complementary requirements on the embodied side of the interface:
fast, history-aware control and efficient goal-directed planning. They do not yet form an
integrated neural-to-action pipeline; connecting them to a neural decoder is a future systems
step.
    Chapter 8 introduces DySta, an efficient multi-frame vision-language-action model.
Standard models either observe only the current frame or incur a rapidly growing context
and repeated computation when past frames are included. DySta separates visual tokens
according to their temporal persistence, retains one copy of static information across time,
and uses a learned recache gate to decide when cached tokens should be refreshed. This
design improves temporal reasoning while reducing both context length and inference
latency.
    Chapter 9 introduces PHIL, a framework for learning graph-search heuristics. Hand-
designed heuristics require domain expertise, while existing learned heuristics can struggle
to retain the structure revealed during search or scale across environments. PHIL learns
an explored-graph memory through imitation learning and uses it to estimate which nodes
should be visited next. Experiments on grids, graphs from several domains, and drone
planning demonstrate its value as a longer-horizon planning component.
    Together, the three parts trace a path from models of neural organization, through repre-
sentations that make brain states communicable, to the control and planning capabilities
required for future closed-loop interaction.




                                             5
Chapter 2

  Prologue — R EP QA: Evaluating the
 Readability of Large Language Models
     in Patient Education Question
               Answering
Chapter 1 argued that language, despite being the default interface between minds and
machines, is a narrow channel: its bandwidth falls well below the rate at which thought
unfolds, and some content resists verbalization altogether. That argument was made largely
from the human-to-machine direction, i.e., the cost a user pays to render an intention into
words before a system can act on it. Before turning to that direction, we first offer one piece
of evidence for the premise of this dissertation from the opposite one, machine-to-human,
where the conditions for language are as favorable as they can be. The speaker is a model
with unlimited patience, broad knowledge, and full control over its own phrasing, and the
message it must convey is ordinary prose rather than a percept or an affective state.
    We study this direction in the setting of patient education question answering, where the
consequences of a message failing to arrive are concrete. Health information is produced by
domain experts but consumed by lay users, so an answer succeeds only if its recipient can
actually read it. This chapter introduces R EP QA, a benchmark that evaluates the readability
of large language models in this setting alongside the informativeness of what they say,
and uses it to ask whether models can answer at a requested reading level, infer the level a
question calls for, and be steered toward readability without losing content. What follows
is offered as an opening illustration rather than an argument: even under these favorable
conditions, having the right content did not guarantee that it reached the reader.




                                              6
2.1      Introduction
Benefiting from extensive training on large-scale corpora, large language models (LLMs)
have demonstrated remarkable reasoning capabilities in solving complex problems [Ope-
nAI et al., 2024a, DeepSeek-AI et al., 2024, Team et al., 2024, Qiu et al., 2025a, Zhao
et al., 2023]. One of the most impactful application areas is healthcare, which demands
strong domain-specific knowledge and a high reliability standard [Singhal et al., 2023,
Thirunavukarasu et al., 2023, Nazi and Peng, 2024]. Previous studies have introduced
benchmarks [Yao et al., 2024, Jin et al., 2019a, Jiang et al., 2024, Wang et al., 2024f]
targeting different levels of medical reasoning tasks.
    Despite their promising performance, the readability of LLM-generated responses, an
essential factor for patient education, i.e., communicating health information to laypeople,
has been largely overlooked and remains systematically underexplored.
      Low-Readability Patient Education Agent                High-Readability Patient Education Agent


            Why is sleep apnea dangerous?                         Why is sleep apnea dangerous?


   Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) leads to interm-        Sleep apnea is dangerous because your breath-
   ittent hypoxia during sleep due to upper airway       ing stops during sleep, which can harm your
   obstruction, increasing cardiovascular risk.          heart and make you very tired during the day.


            Sorry. I still don't understand.                      That sounds bad. I will ask my doctor.




Figure 2.1: Two examples of user interactions with low- and high-readability public health
agents. Medical jargon is underscored for the low-readability agent.

    As shown in Figure 2.1, users without a medical professional background may find low-
readability responses filled with medical jargon difficult to comprehend, whereas a plain-
language answer can convey the same essential information more accessibly. Moreover,
state-of-the-art LLMs are increasingly optimized for complex reasoning [Shao et al., 2024],
raising the question of whether these gains come at the expense of readability. More
advanced reasoning may lead to more technical responses that are harder for lay users to
understand.
    To evaluate the capabilities of LLMs in solving healthcare and medical problems,
multiple benchmarks have been curated from diverse sources and presented in various
formats [Singhal et al., 2023, Liévin et al., 2024, Thirunavukarasu et al., 2023]. For
instance, MedQA [Jin et al., 2021], MedMCQA [Pal et al., 2022], MMLU [Hendrycks
et al., 2020, Wang et al., 2024f], and PubMedQA [Jin et al., 2019a] contain multiple-
choice questions sourced from medical licensing examinations or PubMed articles, while
LiveQA [Abacha et al., 2017] and MedicationQA [Abacha et al., 2019] focus on long-form
question answering for medicine-related problems. Recent efforts have shifted toward more


                                                     7
Table 2.1: The comparison between R EP QA and the existing healthcare benchmark datasets.
               Dataset                        Answer Format                Sources of Questions            Evaluation Metrics           Target Audience
       MedQA [Jin et al., 2021]               Multiple Choice             Medical Examination                Accuracy                 Medical Professionals
     MedMCQA [Pal et al., 2022]               Multiple Choice             Medical Examination                Accuracy                 Medical Professionals
    MMLU [Hendrycks et al., 2020]             Multiple Choice             Medical Examination                Accuracy                      Academic
     PubMedQA [Jin et al., 2019a]             Multiple Choice               PubMed Abstract                  Accuracy                 Clinical Researchers
     LiveQA [Abacha et al., 2017]              Long Answer             National Library of Medicine       Human Evaluation                 Consumers
   MedicationQA [Abacha et al., 2019]          Long Answer              MedlinePlus & DailyMed            Human Evaluation            Medicine Consumers
 JAMA & Medbullets [Chen et al., 2025] Long Answer & Multiple Choice   JAMA Challenge & USMLE           NLG Metrics & Accuracy        Medical Professionals
               R EP QA                         Long Answer             Public Health FAQs & Experts   Readability & Informativeness    Lay Users / Patients




complex clinical tasks requiring multi-step reasoning and decision-making [Yao et al., 2024,
Chen et al., 2025, Nori et al., 2024, Saab et al., 2024], as well as incorporating additional
modalities such as electronic health records [Ou et al., 2025, Bardhan et al., 2022, Kweon
et al., 2024], radiology [Zuo et al., 2025, Liu et al., 2021], and pathology [He et al., 2020,
Lu et al., 2024]. However, these benchmarks mainly evaluate the factual or reasoning
accuracy of LLMs against professional examinations or specialized clinical tasks, rather
than the readability of responses delivered to lay users.
    Beyond evaluation, a related line of research studies how to control the complexity or
reading level of text generated by LLMs. Three main strategies have been explored: (1)
prompt- or instruction-based methods that modulate output difficulty by varying instructions
or providing exemplars [Tran et al., 2024, Malik et al., 2024, Barayan et al., 2024, Pu
and Demberg, 2023]; (2) fine-tuning-based methods, e.g., Chi et al. [2023] fine-tune
language models on same-level paraphrasing datasets, and Ribeiro et al. [2023] employ
PPO [Schulman et al., 2017] with the measured reading level [Kincaid et al., 1975] as a
reward signal; and (3) decoding-based methods that incorporate constraints during text
generation [Ribeiro et al., 2023, Luo et al., 2022]. Several studies have further investigated
the readability of LLM-generated responses regarding certain cancers [Hershenhouse et al.,
2024, Gencer, 2024] or patient education handouts [Swisher et al., 2024]. While most prior
work focuses on text summarization, R EP QA specifically targets the readability of LLMs
in patient education QA tasks.
    In this study, a multidisciplinary team comprised of computer science, nursing, and
public health experts introduced R EP QA to conduct a comprehensive investigation into
the REadability of LLMs in Patient Education question-Answering (QA) tasks. A key
strength of R EP QA lies in its expert-grounded foundation: it comprises a high-quality
test set with 533 QA pairs curated from 27 online public health resources. Different
from prior related benchmarks (Table 2.1), R EP QA focuses on frequently asked health
questions from lay users or patients, and evaluates the readability of generated responses,
an axis that prior healthcare benchmarks largely overlook, rather than only the underlying
factual knowledge of the LLMs. Additionally, we collected a large-scale corpus of 36,060
public health questions to serve as a post-training dataset, supporting the exploration of
readability-enhancement strategies.
    We evaluated 19 LLMs on R EP QA using three readability metrics (Flesch-Kincaid grade


                                                                          8
level [Kincaid et al., 1975], professional-language proportion, and Yngve depth [Yngve,
1960]) and an informativeness metric (the informative cosine between a response and
its reference answer). Our study targeted three capabilities: (i) answering at a specified
reading level, (ii) inferring the appropriate level for a question, and (iii) using readability
as a reward signal. We found that current models mostly missed target levels and poorly
identified appropriate levels, limiting their applicability for public-health agents. We
then benchmarked five existing readability-improvement strategies, including training-free
controls (instruction-based prompting [Ribeiro et al., 2023, Malik et al., 2024], CoT [Wei
et al., 2022], and lookahead decoding [Ribeiro et al., 2023]) and reinforcement-learning
fine-tuning (GRPO [Shao et al., 2024] and DAPO [Yu et al., 2025]). Readability-aware
post-training yielded large gains in readability metrics but could reduce the informativeness
of responses due to over-simplification, underscoring a difficult readability–informativeness
trade-off. This study will offer insights for future work on developing trustworthy healthcare
agents and educational domains [Wang et al., 2024b]. In summary, our contributions are
listed as follows:

• We introduced R EP QA, an expert-crafted benchmark for patient education QA aimed at
  lay users, with three readability metrics (Flesch–Kincaid grade, professional-language
  proportion, and Yngve depth), an informativeness metric (the informative cosine between
  a response and its reference answer), and a large-scale (36,060) expert-reviewed public
  health questions for post-training.

• We conducted a study on 19 LLMs, revealing that most models failed to (i) generate at
  requested reading levels and (ii) identify an appropriate level for a given question.

• We benchmarked a range of existing strategies for improving LLM readability, span-
  ning training-free control (prompting, chain-of-thought, and lookahead decoding) and
  reinforcement-learning fine-tuning (GRPO and DAPO). Readability improved substan-
  tially, but often at the expense of informativeness, highlighting a challenging readabil-
  ity–informativeness trade-off.


2.2     Results
2.2.1    Overview of R EP QA
R EP QA is a benchmark for systematically evaluating the readability of LLMs in patient
education question answering. It comprises a high-quality collection of patient education
QA pairs, each with an expert-grounded reference answer, together with a large-scale set of
unlabeled questions for training.
    Figure 2.2 illustrates the overall dataset construction and evaluation pipeline: the dataset
is curated through expert review, while the evaluation involves both readability assessment

Figure 2.2: Overview of the R EP QA benchmark. Top: dataset construction — patient-
education QA pairs are collected from 27 online public health resources and filtered by
domain experts to suit lay users, yielding 533 expert-grounded QA pairs (each with an
auto-generated multiple-choice counterpart) that span four categories (Suggestion, Fact,
Definition, and Rationale). Bottom: evaluation — given a question, an LLM agent
generates a free-form response, which is scored by three readability metrics (Flesch–
Kincaid grade, professional language, and Yngve depth) and an informativeness metric (the
informative cosine between the response and the expert reference answer).


and an informativeness measure against the reference answers. The construction procedure,
evaluation metrics, and study design are detailed in the Methods (Section 2.4).
    The evaluation set of R EP QA contains 533 QA pairs curated from 27 authoritative
online public health resources and reviewed by domain experts in nursing, public health, and
clinical communication. Each question is phrased in plain language, without user profiles,
and is accompanied by a reference answer. As shown in Table 2.2, the QA pairs span four
categories that capture common types of public health inquiries from lay users: Suggestion
(questions seeking advice), Fact (questions requesting objective, verifiable information),
Definition (questions asking for explanations of medical terms or conditions), and Rationale
(questions seeking justifications behind medical guidance). To enable informativeness
assessment of free-form responses, each QA pair includes an expert-grounded reference
answer, against which a generated response is compared via the informative cosine (IC).
In addition, R EP QA provides a large-scale collection of 36,060 expert-reviewed public
health questions spanning topics such as sleep, exercise, smoking, weight, diet, HIV care,
mental health, cardiovascular disease, diabetes, cholesterol, and SOGI, supporting the
exploration of readability-enhancement strategies such as reinforcement learning with
verifiable readability rewards.
    Using R EP QA, we studied three research questions concerning LLMs’ ability to adapt,
assess, and optimize readability: (RQ1) can LLMs generate responses at a specified reading
level? (RQ2) can LLMs determine what readability is needed for a given question? and

(RQ3) can readability serve as a reward signal? We evaluated 19 LLMs, including 5 propri-
etary models and 14 open-source models, alongside five readability-enhancement strategies.
The full experimental setup, including the list of evaluated models and configurations, is
provided in Section 2.4.5.


2.2.2        LLMs struggle to generate responses at a specified reading level
People with different educational backgrounds have varying health literacy, so a healthcare
agent should be able to adapt the readability of its answers to a requested level (RQ1). We
set a target score, i.e., 6 for the Flesch–Kincaid (FK) grade level and 0 for the professional-
language (PL) score, and prompted each model to answer the queries accordingly. π=6
approximates a 5th-grade level intended to be broadly accessible, and ρ=0 denotes the
absence of medical jargon. As shown in Table 2.3, most models struggled to hit the target,
often overshooting complexity or, when simplifying, omitting qualifiers. Across the four
categories, definition was the easiest, with the lowest readability scores. A more detailed
analysis of Table 2.3 is provided in Supplementary Section ??.
    To further probe readability control, we evaluated a set of representative models, span-
ning non-thinking models (LLaMA 3.1-8B, Qwen-3-30B) and thinking models (DeepSeek-
R1-Distill-Qwen, Qwen-3-30B-Thinking, GPT-4.1), focusing on their ability to match
requested reading levels. We set four target levels, i.e., 5th grade, middle school, high
school, and college, corresponding to FK grade levels of 6, 9, 12, and 15.
    As shown in Figure 2.3(a,b), all models generally followed the intended direction
(Spearman ρ ≈ 0.34 ∼ 0.57) but remained poorly calibrated: their FK grades consistently

2.2.3    LLMs poorly identify the readability required for a question
We next examined whether poor control stems from a weak understanding of readability
(RQ2). We first tested whether models can recognize the reading level of existing text:
we sampled 200 generated responses per bin (Elementary, Middle School, High School,
College; mapped from FK grade) and asked models to classify the bin. As shown in
Figure 2.3(c), accuracy peaked at Middle School, was moderate at Elementary, and col-
lapsed at High School and College. This systematic bias toward lower buckets mirrored the
undershoot observed for readability control.
    We then studied whether LLMs understand differences in the medical knowledge
required by a question. For each question in R EP QA, we prompted GPT-4o to generate
two counterparts that vary only in the required domain knowledge (Medium and High);
examples are provided in Supplementary Section ??. Each model then classified the
resulting variant. As shown in Figure 2.3(d), performance was uneven across systems,
e.g., LLaMA 3.1-8B was relatively strong on Medium but weak on Original, whereas
Qwen-3-30B-Thinking excelled on Original yet remained low on High. Across models,
detection of High difficulty was uniformly poor, indicating limited sensitivity to advanced
domain knowledge and helping explain the tendency to default to overly simple outputs.

Table 2.4: Readability-control strategies on the R EP QA metric suite, per question type. For
each base model we compared training-free controls – Prompt (instruction-based control),
CoT (chain-of-thought), and Lookahead (readability-guided lookahead decoding, Ribeiro
et al., 2023) – against RL fine-tuning (DAPO/GRPO). PL = Professional Language (↓),
FK = Flesch–Kincaid Grade (target ≈ 6), YD = Yngve Depth (↓, raw), IC = Informative
Cosine (↑). Prompting and lookahead improve word-level readability (FK) but not syntactic
complexity (YD); RL improves all axes while best retaining informativeness (IC). Within
each base model, bold marks the best and underline the second-best value per column.


2.2.4     Readability can serve as a reward signal, but at an accuracy cost
Reinforcement learning has shown promise for steering LLMs toward preferred behaviors.
We therefore studied whether readability can act as a useful reward signal (RQ3). We opti-
mized readability using the reward in Eq. 2.4 within two reinforcement-learning algorithms,
GRPO [Shao et al., 2024] and DAPO [Yu et al., 2025], the latter improving upon GRPO
with decoupled clipping and dynamic sampling (Methods, Section 2.4). As training-free
baselines we included three readability-control methods from Ribeiro et al. [2023]: Prompt
(instruction-based control, prompting the base model for simple, plain-language answers),
CoT (chain-of-thought prompting), and Lookahead (a decoding method that at each step
estimates the readability of candidate continuations via a short greedy lookahead and biases
generation toward the target grade level). All three were applied to the base model with no
fine-tuning. Results are reported in Table 2.4.
    The training-free controls traded off differently across the readability axes. Prompt
sharply lowered the word-level grade (FK) and jargon (PL), but raised syntactic complexity
(YD) and eroded informativeness (IC); Lookahead pulled FK toward the target while best
preserving IC among the non-RL methods, yet left jargon and syntax largely unchanged
because its guidance optimizes FK alone; and CoT produced less readable text with no
meaningful gain in informativeness. In contrast, RL fine-tuning (GRPO/DAPO) was the
only approach that improved all four axes at once – lowering FK, PL, and YD while retaining


                                                                      14
the highest IC – since its reward jointly targets word choice, syntax, and faithfulness. Both
GRPO and DAPO improved readability; among them, DAPO yielded the largest gains,
reducing the grade level by 41.4% on average and the professional-language score by
73.3%. Moreover, DAPO outperformed vanilla GRPO on professional-language readability
(53.4% average reduction) while maintaining a comparable grade level (3.0% difference).
Although RL fine-tuning largely retained informativeness (IC) at the aggregate level, the
drive toward simpler text can still cost content fidelity: as the case study (Section 2.2.5) and
the expert evaluation below show, the simplified responses sometimes omitted key facts,
especially on Suggestion and Fact, highlighting the difficulty of simplifying without losing
content.

Expert evaluation We performed a manual inspection of the responses from Qwen2.5-7B
and LLaMA3.1-8B, both post-trained via DAPO. Specifically, we presented questions to 8
domain experts in a blind evaluation, in which experts were asked to select the superior
response from a pair of randomized outputs. Our results showed that in 75% of the cases,
experts favored the responses from LLaMA3.1-8B over those from Qwen2.5-7B. This
discrepancy with the metric-based ranking in Table 2.4 suggested that static readability
metrics may deviate from human perception.


2.2.5         Case study: improved readability at the expense of informative-
              ness
R EP QA revealed that, although GRPO/DAPO with a readability-based reward markedly
improved readability, it can reduce the informativeness of responses.

    To illustrate this trade-off, Figure 2.4 presented two cases where DAPO yielded simpler
text but missed key facts relative to the base model. In particular, the DAPO model tended
to over-simplify, compressing lists and collapsing categories, so that only a salient term


                                                                                15
was retained (e.g., craving for alcohol use disorder; statins for cholesterol drugs) while
other classes (e.g., bile-acid sequestrants, fibrates, niacin, PCSK9 inhibitors) were merged
into a single undifferentiated sentence, leading to information loss.


2.3     Discussion
In this study, we examined a critical yet underexplored factor in developing effective patient
education agents, i.e., the readability of LLM-generated responses, through R EP QA, a
benchmark of expert-grounded patient education QA pairs. R EP QA comprises 533 QA
pairs curated from 27 authoritative online resources and reviewed by professionals in
nursing, public health, and clinical communication, together with a large-scale collection of
36,060 public health questions used to explore strategies for improving readability. Unlike
prior healthcare benchmarks, which primarily assess the factual or reasoning accuracy of
LLMs against professional examinations or specialized clinical tasks, R EP QA is designed
around the needs of lay users and couples three readability metrics (Flesch–Kincaid grade,
professional-language proportion, and Yngve depth) with an informativeness metric that
measures how much of an expert-grounded reference answer a response conveys.
    Our evaluation of 19 LLMs revealed a consistent and important limitation of current
systems: they struggled both to follow targeted readability instructions and to recognize
the reading level required by a question or exhibited by a response. Models generally
adjusted readability in the right direction but remained poorly calibrated in absolute terms,
systematically undershooting higher target grade levels, and their ability to detect high-
difficulty questions and high-grade text was uniformly weak. Together, these observations
pointed to a gap between raw reasoning ability and effective communication: stronger
reasoning does not automatically translate into responses that lay users can understand.
    We further benchmarked several existing readability-enhancement strategies spanning
test-time control (standard prompting, chain-of-thought, and readability-guided lookahead
decoding) and reinforcement-learning fine-tuning (GRPO and DAPO). We found that
test-time controls traded off unevenly across readability axes, e.g., prompting lowered
word-level grade and jargon but increased syntactic complexity and eroded informativeness,
whereas reinforcement learning was the only family of methods that improved word choice,
syntax, and faithfulness jointly. Among them, DAPO yielded the largest readability gains,
reducing the grade level by 41.4% and the professional-language score by 73.3% on
average. However, these gains can come at the expense of informativeness, particularly
for Suggestion and Fact questions, as models over-simplified by compressing lists and
collapsing categories. This exposed a fundamental readability–informativeness trade-off
that must be navigated when building accessible healthcare agents.
    A further observation concerns the evaluation of readability itself. In a blind expert
evaluation, domain experts favored responses from a model that the static metrics ranked


                                             16
lower, indicating that automated readability metrics, while useful and scalable, may diverge
from human perception of clarity and usefulness. This motivates future work on readability
measures and reward signals that better align with expert and patient judgments, as well as
methods that preserve essential clinical content while simplifying language, for example by
substituting jargon with plain-language equivalents rather than discarding it.
    In conclusion, building user-centered public health agents requires explicitly optimizing
for readability without sacrificing informativeness, an objective that current LLMs and
existing enhancement strategies only partially achieve. By providing an expert-grounded
benchmark, complementary readability and informativeness metrics, and a large-scale
training corpus, R EP QA offers a foundation for studying and improving this trade-off, and
we hope it will guide the development of more accessible and trustworthy LLM-powered
healthcare agents.
    Limitations. We focused on English content and lay users in the U.S. healthcare context.
As such, the generalizability of our findings to other languages, cultures, or health systems
remains limited. In addition, the readability metrics adopted here, although standard and
complementary, are imperfect proxies for human comprehension, as highlighted by our
expert evaluation.


2.4     Methods
In this section, we detail the construction of R EP QA, the evaluation metrics, the readability-
enhancement strategies we benchmarked, the study design behind our three research
questions, and the experimental setup.


2.4.1    Dataset construction
Expert-grounded patient education QA. We collected a diverse set of publicly available
healthcare-related QA pairs from 27 authoritative online resources covering a broad range of
public health topics, including general health, cardiovascular disease, cholesterol, diabetes,
HIV, diet, exercise, alcohol, smoking, sleep, mental health, sexual health, and health equity.
Sources were selected for their accessibility to lay audiences and their coverage of frequently
asked questions by the general public. All the links can be found in Supplementary
Section ??. For the evaluation dataset, domain experts further manually filtered out QA
pairs containing overly technical content, following national, authoritative research and
clinical guidelines [Doody and Noonan, 2016, Chiang-Hanisko et al., 2016, Corry et al.,
2013]. Questions are phrased in plain language and do not include user profiles. A detailed
description of the curation pipeline and the reviewing team’s composition is provided
in Supplementary Section ??. The resulting evaluation dataset includes 533 QA pairs
spanning four categories, i.e., Suggestion, Fact, Definition, and Rationale, as summarized
in Table 2.2:


                                              17
Table 2.5: Examples and distribution of question categories in the collected training datasets.
A single question can be categorized into multiple categories.
   Category       Example                                                                                                      Proportion
     Sleep        How does sleep apnea during pregnancy potentially affect newborns?                                             8.1%
    Exercise      What are some effective strategies for improving family health through physical activities?                    6.3%
   Smoking        What are some effective strategies individuals can use to quit smoking and support a smoke-free lifestyle?     4.5%
    Weight        How is body mass index (BMI) calculated, and what factors can affect its accuracy?                             7.2%

   HIV Care       How can gay men effectively manage their risk of HIV?                                                         14.4%

   Diabetes       How do BMI categories relate to the risk of type 2 diabetes across different age groups?                       5.2%
